2026-08-07T18:05Z · test · NOTE · the headline is #44 and #45, both p0, and BOTH are score arithmetic that decides who gets shortlisted · `ScreeningResult.ComputeTotal` (ScreeningResult.cs:87-91) builds the maximum by iterating the SUPPLIED scores, not the rubric's criteria, and `screen_applicant` validates neither completeness nor distinctness. Priced p0 under the domain rule rather than the skill's table, which would call a wrong number on a read surface p1: this product decides who gets a job, so a defect that silently changes rank is p0 even when it looks cosmetic. ADR-0004 keeps an `unresolved` criterion inside the maximum precisely so a vague CV cannot shrink the yardstick — omitting the criterion achieves that outcome more cheaply, and nothing checks.

2026-08-08T00:32Z · ship · NOTE · THE FINDING OF THIS TICK · #54 fixes the score-inflation p0 correctly and then reopens the same class one level down. Its completeness check is `rubric.Criteria.Where(c => !scoredCriterionIds.Contains(c.Id))` — **set-cover, not exact-cover**. It proves every criterion appears at least once and rejects nothing for appearing twice; `rows` is built by a plain `foreach` over the model's `ScoredCriterion[]` with no de-duplication, and `criteriaByName` is `OrdinalIgnoreCase`, so `"Mentoring"`/`"mentoring"` collapse to one criterion without reading as a duplicate. Because the PR moves `max` off the supplied set onto the rubric's weight table while `total` is still summed per row, the invariant `total <= max` — which held UNCONDITIONALLY before this PR — is now breakable. On seeded rubric v1 (5,4,4,3,2 → 90): an honest 60/90 with `Production Python experience` repeated at 5 reads **85/90 = 94.4%** and outranks the honest 80/90 candidate from issue #44's own table; the same input read 85/115 = 73.9% before. `CvHighlighting.Segment` `.Distinct(…)`s the covering criterion names, so a duplicated citation renders NO doubled highlight — the only tell is a repeated row next to a total that is already wrong. Fix named with its red-before-green: `ComputeTotal([Score(A,5), Score(A,5)], {A:5, B:1})` returns `total 50, max 30` on THIS head, so the assertion `total <= max` is red now. That the fix for a rank-inflation bug can itself widen rank inflation is the argument for adversarial review that no gate makes.

2026-08-08T00:32Z · ship · NOTE · what the review CLEARED, recorded so the next tick does not re-litigate it · the full diff greps ZERO on all six protected patterns — `RequiresApproval`, `Permissions.`, `AddPlenipoRole`, `HasQueryFilter`, `consent`, `impact` — so the scheduled task's extra hold on consent and impact-report paths does not fire, and neither surface exists yet (`HiringModule.cs:21` records both as epics 2–7). No assertion deleted, no golden eval loosened (`hiring-screening-is-not-gated.json` untouched, and its `replyMustContain` was already empty). The four modified fixtures are a forced precondition repair, not a weakening. 2026-08-08T00:32Z · ship · NOTE · a design decision is being made with NO ADR behind it, and the PR cites one that does not say it · "refuse a partial set rather than back-fill missing criteria as `unresolved`" is attributed to ADR-0004. ADR-0004 is about the APPROVAL GATE and says nothing about unresolved semantics or completeness; the "unresolved is a judgement nobody made" reading lives only in `ScreeningResult`'s XML docs, a code comment and a PR body — all three invisible after merge. The reasoning is sound and consistent with ADR-0001/0002's posture, which is exactly why it should be ADR-0012 rather than folklore: this is product-fairness policy on a product that decides who gets a job. Second-order consequence the human should weigh: a candidate the model cannot score completely becomes UNSCREENABLE, and `AdvanceCandidatesAsync`/`RejectCandidateAsync` both throw without a live screening, so they stall in the pipeline with nothing on their record saying an attempt was made and refused — the audit row for a refusal is `success: true, error: null`. Stalling is the right DIRECTION to fail here; it is not free.

2026-08-08T17:08Z · deliver · NOTE · **THE FINDING — the burn is real, and the regulatory half is worse than "misattributed"** · three review rounds, the 16:02Z tick and the 16:39Z triage comment all priced option (c) on a claim nobody had ever run. It is now run, and **both halves confirm**. (1) After a `hiring-recruiter` — holding `Permissions.ManageApprovals` and **not** `tools.hiring.advance_candidates` — POSTs `/api/chat/approvals/{id}/approve` on a talent lead's parked `advance_candidates`, the row ends terminally **`Failed`** with `ResolvedByUserId` stamped. Nothing in the platform assigns `ApprovalStatus.Pending` after creation, so the lead's decision **cannot be re-approved by anyone** and must be proposed again from a fresh chat turn: an unauthorized click is not a no-op, it is a **denial of service against the only role permitted to make the call**. (2) `/api/platform/ai-decisions` lists that same row with `oversight: "approved"` and a non-empty `decidedBy` — asserted by locating the entry **by id**, not by grepping the payload for the word. `DisclosureEndpoints.cs:129` maps every non-`Rejected` survivor to `"approved"`, deliberately (`"a failed execution is still an approved decision"`), which is right for a genuine approval whose tool threw and wrong for this. So the ADMT disclosure — the record that exists to answer *who decided* — states that **the recruiter approved an advance** that was never written and that they were never permitted to make. On a product that decides who gets a job, that is a false entry in the regulatory record, and it is the strongest argument yet against simply living with the shim.

2026-08-08T17:08Z · deliver · NOTE · the measurement trap, recorded because it would have produced the opposite answer · the first run of this assertion reported **`Pending`** and would have read as *"the decision survives, the burn is folklore, option (c) is free."* It was an artifact: `IApprovalStore.GetAsync` was called on the **same scope that created the row**, so EF handed back the tracked instance with its constructed status while the approve had committed `Failed` from a different request scope. Re-read through a **fresh** scope it is `Failed`, and the disclosure endpoint — reading through its own request scope — agrees independently. Both readings were seen before either was trusted, which is why this is L1/L3 and not a reading of code text. Anyone re-verifying #51 will hit this trap, and the comment in `AdvanceTests.cs` now says so at the call site.

2026-08-08T17:20Z · ship · NOTE · **THE NEW FINDING: the setting that converts the loop's own journal commit into a merge block has a name, and no previous tick named it** · `gh api repos/abrahamFerga/hireworthy/branches/main/protection/required_status_checks` returns **`strict: true`** — GitHub's *"require branches to be up to date before merging"*. That is the whole mechanism. With `strict` on, **any** commit to `main` sets `mergeStateStatus=BEHIND` on **every** open PR, and vendored `merge-gate.mjs:198` treats `BEHIND` as terminal alongside `DIRTY`/`BLOCKED`. Prior ticks measured the symptom correctly (13, then 14, now 22–35 commits of pure journal) but attributed it to the journal commits alone; the journal is only half — `strict: true` is the half that makes a `TICKS.md` commit indistinguishable from a real conflict. Re-proved at L1 this tick with a positive control on each merge-base rather than on `main`'s tip: `git diff --name-only $(git merge-base <head> origin/main) origin/main` returns exactly `TICKS.md` for `b0a53fc`, `1e00add` and `88ecd4c`, and the same command with `':!TICKS.md'` returns empty — the unfiltered form was seen returning a path FIRST, so the exclusion pathspec is live and not silently matching nothing. Behind counts: `#38` 35 · `#41` 35 · `#42` 29 · `#54` 22 · `#56` 25. **This is named as a mechanism, NOT as a recommendation:** disabling `strict` is a branch-protection change, it is a human's to make, and it would be weakening a gate to move a PR — the exact thing this run is forbidden to do. The honest framing for a human is that there are three levers and all three are theirs: decide `#42`, update the branches, or change `strict`.

2026-08-08T22:39Z · test · NOTE · a near-miss that would have skipped a REAL sweep, recorded so the next tick does not repeat it · this session's checkout is `fix/51-approve-rechecks-tool-permission` at `6beb0b5`, carrying **seven unmerged code commits** that do touch `src` (`0a21096` alone: `HiringTools.cs` +55, `AdvanceTests.cs` +75, `ManifestGuardTests.cs` +18). `git log --oneline origin/main..HEAD` returned **empty** — which reads as "those commits are already on main" and would have made the tree-identity result look like a contradiction to be explained away. It was a **PowerShell artifact**: `Select-Object -First` earlier in the same command block raises `StopUpstreamCommandsException` and truncated the later statement's output. The authoritative check disagrees — `git merge-base --is-ancestor 6beb0b5 origin/main` exits **1**, so the seven commits are branch-only and unmerged, and the No-op stands. Rule for the next tick: settle "is this merged?" with `merge-base --is-ancestor`, never with an empty range-log inside a multi-statement PowerShell block.

2026-08-09T02:25Z · ship · NOTE · the round-4 finding, which is worse than ADR-0014 states and is the reason the verdict is `escalate` · `ApprovalStore.ListPendingAsync` filters on `Status == Pending` and NOTHING else, and `GET /api/chat/approvals` applies no per-tool permission filter — so the recruiter's queue SHOWS them the talent lead's parked advance, with an Approve button. Clicking it runs `TryBeginExecutionAsync` (flips `Pending` to `Executing`, stamps the recruiter as resolver) BEFORE the tool is invoked; the shim then throws, the endpoint writes `Failed`, and `Failed` is terminal (`git grep ApprovalStatus.Pending` at the tag: seven hits, exactly one an assignment, and it is a field initializer). `DisclosureEndpoints.cs` maps the survivor to `"approved"` with `decidedBy = ResolvedByDisplay`. So one click by a recruiter doing their job permanently destroys a decision only the talent lead may make, and records the recruiter in the ADMT disclosure as having approved it. Neither remedy #51 listed has this property — both refuse before the transition. The trade is a privilege escalation that writes versus a one-click destruction of a parked hiring decision plus a false regulatory entry. Both real; which is worse is not an agent's call.

2026-08-12T22:19Z · ship · NOTE · **a real defect this tick found: `trusted_pr_gates` cannot be evaluated on Windows, and no agent here may fix it** · `merge-gate.mjs:279` fetches the protected-base `pr-gates.mjs` via `gh api -H 'Accept: application/vnd.github.raw+json' …`, and the `gh` helper at `:42` spawns with `shell: process.platform === 'win32'`. Under `shell: true` Node concatenates argv **without escaping** — its own `DEP0190` warning says exactly this — so the header splits at its space and `gh` exits 1 with `accepts 1 arg(s), received 2`. Reproduced at **L1** by re-running that identical `spawnSync` standalone; the same call from a shell with the header quoted succeeds and returns the script, which is what makes it look like an auth or network problem. The thrown message is then truncated by `.split('\n')[0]`, so it surfaces as `failed:` with nothing after the colon — the diagnostic is discarded at the exact point it would be useful. Consequence: for **every** control-plane PR (`#38`, `#41`, `#56` here) the protected-base evaluator is skipped and replaced by a hard failure, and the script exits **non-zero on every tick on this machine**, which is why this tick is `Blocked` and not `Approval-required`. It is *not* the binding constraint on any individual PR — all three fail other gates independently — but a merger that cannot execute its own contract is broken infrastructure, and the skill says to name that rather than report healthy waiting. **Not repaired here:** the fix is one line under `.github/`, and this loop is barred from editing its own gates. It is also vendored from the marketplace, so it is fleet-wide, not a hireworthy defect.
